Engagement reflects the strength and activity of the relationship rather than the member's financial value, potential opportunity, satisfaction, loyalty, or intent. How engaged is the member in their relationship with the organization?",
"Member Individuality Profile":"Represents a structured and consistently captured set of information about characteristics that distinguish individual members and are relevant and appropriate within the context of the financial relationship. This may include preferences, financial aspirations and priorities, communication and relationship preferences, relevant interests and values, observable behaviours, and other factors that may influence how a member engages with the organization and makes financial decisions. The Member Individuality Profile seeks to improve understanding of meaningful differences between members while deliberately limiting information to that which is appropriate and relevant in the normal course of business. It recognizes that any structured representation necessarily provides only a partial view of the complexity and uniqueness of an individual. What relevant and appropriate information do we know about what makes this member distinct as an individual?",
"Decomposition Component Requirement":'For each required component, identify the information that would ideally be needed to adequately represent it, independent of the information currently available.',
"Semantic Classification":'Purpose: Establish a consistent understanding of what information is contained within the available dataset. Canonical Question:What variables are contained in the dataset, and what do they represent?',
"Problem Decomposition":"Break the analytical problem into the distinct conceptual components that must be understood or represented to adequately address it. Canonical Question: What components are required to adequately represent the analytical problem?",
"Decomposition Component Requirement":'For each required component, identify the information that would ideally be needed to adequately represent it, independent of the information currently available.',
"Variable Mapping":"Connect the conceptual information requirements of the problem to the variables that actually exist in the dataset. Canonical question: Which available variables provide evidence relevant to each required component or information requirement?",
"Gap Assessment":"Evaluate the extent to which the available data satisfies the information requirements identified through Problem Decomposition, explicitly identifying areas that are sufficiently represented, partially represented, or missing. Do we have sufficient information to adequately represent each required component of the analytical problem, and what important information is missing?",
'Data Ingestion & Reproducibility': "Establish a consistent and repeatable process for sourcing, loading, and combining the data required for analysis and modeling. The process should clearly identify source systems, extraction logic, joins, filters, observation periods, and other material decisions required to recreate the dataset. Where manual steps are necessary, they should be explicitly documented with the intention of reducing or automating them where practical. Canonical Question: Can the modeling dataset be recreated consistently from the identified source data?",
'Structural Validation': 'Confirm that the assembled dataset has the expected structure before substantive analysis or transformation begins. This includes validating the unit of observation, unique identifiers, row and column counts, data types, duplicates, joins, expected categories, and other structural characteristics. Unexpected changes should be identified and understood rather than silently incorporated into the analytical process Canonical Question: Is the dataset structurally consistent with what we expect it to represent?',
'Data Cleaning': 'Identify and address data conditions that could prevent variables from being reliably interpreted or analyzed. This includes missing values, invalid or impossible values, inconsistent formats, duplicates, incorrect data types, unexpected categories, and other observable data issues. Cleaning decisions should preserve meaningful information where possible and be documented so that modifications to the source information are understood and reproducible. Canonical Question: What must be corrected, standardized, excluded, or explicitly accepted before the data can be reliably analyzed?',
'Temporal Alignment': 'Ensure that observations and variables are aligned to a consistent analytical point in time and that historical measures represent clearly defined and comparable periods. Particular attention should be given to observation dates, lookback windows, reporting periods, and information that may not have been available at the intended point of analysis. Temporal alignment should prevent future information from inadvertently influencing historical analysis or model development. Canonical Question: Does each observation represent information that was consistently available at the intended point in time?',
'Modeling Dataset Construction': 'Construct the analysis-ready dataset that will serve as the controlled input to Exploratory Data Analysis and subsequent modeling activities. This includes applying the established population, observation, inclusion, exclusion, and variable-selection rules necessary to define the analytical dataset while retaining identifiers and supporting metadata where appropriate. The resulting dataset should provide a stable and reproducible analytical starting point rather than embedding model-specific transformations prematurely. Canonical Question: What is the controlled and reproducible dataset that will serve as the foundation for subsequent analysis and modeling?',
'No Imputation': '''No Imputation is appropriate when the selected analytical method can natively handle missing values or when preserving missingness is preferable to introducing an artificial value. For example, scikit-learn LinearRegression does not accept NaN values directly, whereas HistGradientBoostingClassifier and HistGradientBoostingRegressor can handle missing values natively. This approach preserves the original information, avoids introducing assumptions, and can be particularly useful when missingness itself may be meaningful. The primary disadvantage is that it is not supported by all algorithms and may therefore restrict model selection, while algorithms that do support missing values may differ in how they interpret and use them.''',
'Zero Imputation': '''Zero Imputation is appropriate when a missing value has a defensible semantic interpretation of zero, absence, or no activity, such as when utilization is missing because the underlying product is not held. It is simple, transparent, reproducible, and can accurately preserve the meaning of structural absence. The primary risk is that zero and unknown are fundamentally different conditions, and treating unknown values as zero can materially distort distributions and relationships within the data.''',
'Constant / Category Imputation': '''Constant or Category Imputation is appropriate when missingness can reasonably be represented as a distinct state such as Unknown, Not Available, or Not Applicable, particularly for categorical variables. It explicitly preserves the existence of missingness, is simple to interpret, and allows a model to distinguish missing observations from existing categories. The approach creates an artificial category that may not represent a genuine underlying population and may cause the model to learn patterns associated with data collection or operational processes rather than meaningful characteristics.''',
'Mean Imputation': '''Mean Imputation is appropriate for numeric variables with relatively symmetric distributions and limited missingness where the population mean provides a reasonable representation of a typical value. It is simple, computationally inexpensive, reproducible, and preserves the observed mean reasonably well. It reduces variance, weakens relationships between variables, is sensitive to outliers, and can create an artificial concentration of observations around the mean.''',
'Median Imputation': '''Median Imputation is appropriate for numeric variables with skewed distributions, influential outliers, or situations where the median provides a more representative typical value than the mean. It is simple, reproducible, and considerably more robust to extreme values than mean imputation. It reduces variability, does not incorporate relationships with other variables, and can create an artificial concentration of observations at the median when missingness is substantial.''',
'Mode Imputation': '''Mode Imputation is appropriate for categorical or discrete variables where a meaningful dominant category exists and the amount of missing information is relatively limited. It is simple, computationally inexpensive, and ensures that imputed observations remain within an existing valid category. It artificially increases the prevalence of the dominant category and can distort categorical distributions or conceal meaningful patterns in missingness.''',
'Group-Based Imputation': '''Group-Based Imputation is appropriate when the expected value of a variable differs meaningfully across known and defensible groups, allowing a group-specific mean, median, mode, or other representative value to be used instead of a population-wide value. It incorporates contextual information and can provide more representative estimates than global imputation methods. It requires defensible group definitions and sufficient observations within each group, and can reinforce existing group differences, introduce bias, or create leakage when groups are defined using inappropriate information.''',
'Forward Fill / Backward Fill': '''Forward Fill or Backward Fill is primarily appropriate for longitudinal or time-series data where an adjacent observation can reasonably represent the missing period. It preserves information specific to the same entity rather than substituting a population-level estimate and can be effective for variables that change gradually. It assumes temporal continuity and can propagate outdated or inappropriate information across periods, while backward filling can be particularly problematic if it introduces information from the future into an earlier observation.''',
'Interpolation': '''Interpolation is appropriate for ordered or time-series observations where values between known observations can reasonably be estimated from surrounding observations. It uses the structure and trajectory of the observed data and can preserve temporal patterns more effectively than simple statistical imputation. It assumes continuity between observations and can create artificial trends, making it inappropriate for variables that change abruptly, are categorical, or lack a meaningful ordering.''',
'KNN Imputation': '''KNN Imputation is appropriate when observations that are similar across available characteristics are reasonably expected to have similar values for the missing variable. It uses multivariate relationships and local patterns rather than relying solely on the distribution of the variable being imputed, potentially producing more representative estimates than simple statistical methods. It can be computationally expensive, is sensitive to feature scaling, distance measures, and the selected number of neighbours, and can become less effective or interpretable in high-dimensional datasets.''',
'Regression / Predictive Imputation': '''Regression or Predictive Imputation is appropriate when a variable with missing values has sufficiently strong and stable relationships with other observed variables to support estimation. It uses relationships within the available data and can produce more individualized estimates than simple statistical imputation. It introduces additional model assumptions, can create an unjustified impression of precision, may reduce natural variability, and can propagate modeling errors, overfit, or introduce leakage if inappropriate predictors are used.''',
'Multiple Imputation': '''Multiple Imputation is appropriate when uncertainty surrounding missing values is analytically important, particularly in statistical inference, and generates multiple plausible versions of missing values rather than relying on a single estimate. It explicitly incorporates uncertainty associated with missing information and generally provides more statistically principled estimates than single-value imputation. It is computationally and conceptually more complex, requires additional assumptions and workflow management, and may provide limited practical benefit for many predictive machine-learning applications.''',
'Missingness Indicator': '''A Missingness Indicator is appropriate when the fact that information is missing may itself contain useful information and creates a separate binary variable identifying whether the original value was missing. It preserves information about missingness even when the underlying variable is subsequently imputed and allows a model to determine whether absence itself contains useful signal. It increases dimensionality and may cause the model to learn operational, system, or data-collection artifacts rather than meaningful underlying behaviour, and it does not itself resolve the missing value when an algorithm requires complete data.''',
'Row Exclusion': '''Row Exclusion is appropriate when a relatively small number of observations are missing information considered essential for analysis and their removal is unlikely to materially alter the analytical population. It avoids introducing artificial values or assumptions through imputation and is straightforward to implement and explain. It reduces sample size and can introduce selection bias when missingness is systematically related to characteristics of the excluded observations, with the risk increasing as the proportion of excluded records grows.''',
'Variable Exclusion': '''Variable Exclusion is appropriate when a variable has substantial or unreliable missingness and its expected analytical value does not justify the assumptions required to impute it. It simplifies the dataset and model, avoids potentially unreliable imputation, and can improve robustness when the variable contributes little reliable information. It can remove useful predictive or explanatory information, and exclusion based solely on an arbitrary missingness threshold may discard variables whose observed information remains valuable.''',
'Univariate Analysis': '''Univariate Analysis examines individual variables independently to understand their fundamental characteristics and behaviour. This includes assessing distributions, central tendency, variability, range, cardinality, sparsity, skewness, and the presence of unusual or extreme observations where relevant. The objective is to understand what each variable represents statistically before considering its relationship with other information. Canonical Question: What are the important characteristics and distributional properties of each variable?''',
'Bivariate Analysis': '''Bivariate Analysis examines the relationship between pairs of variables to identify associations, dependencies, differences, and potentially meaningful patterns. The appropriate method depends on the variable types and may include correlation, categorical association, group comparisons, or examination of functional and monotonic relationships. Observed relationships should be treated as evidence of association rather than causation unless the analytical design supports a stronger conclusion. Canonical Question: What meaningful relationships or associations exist between individual variables?''',
'Multivariate Analysis': '''Multivariate Analysis examines how multiple variables behave collectively to identify structure, relationships, and patterns that may not be apparent through individual or pairwise analysis. Techniques may be used to explore dimensional structure, combinations of related information, latent patterns, or natural groupings within the data without necessarily committing those techniques to the final model. The objective is to understand the broader information structure of the dataset and how variables interact when considered together. Canonical Question: What structure, relationships, or patterns emerge when multiple variables are considered collectively?''',
'Redundancy & Information Analysis': '''Redundancy and Information Analysis evaluates the extent to which variables provide distinct, overlapping, repetitive, or limited information. This includes identifying highly related variables, near-constant information, repeated representations across timeframes or categories, mathematically related measures, and groups of variables that may largely represent the same underlying phenomenon. The objective is not necessarily to remove variables at this stage, but to understand where the apparent volume of available information may exceed the amount of genuinely distinct information contained within the dataset. Canonical Question: Where does the dataset contain distinct, overlapping, redundant, or limited information?''',
'Component Analysis': '''Component Analysis evaluates the available data in the context of the conceptual components established through Problem Decomposition. It examines whether each component is meaningfully represented by the available variables, how information within each component behaves, and whether empirical patterns are consistent with the intended conceptual interpretation. This provides an opportunity to challenge or refine the original decomposition based on what is learned from the actual data rather than assuming the conceptual structure is correct. Canonical Question: Does the empirical information available for each conceptual component behave in a way that supports the intended analytical construct?''',
'EDA Findings & Implications': '''EDA Findings and Implications consolidates the material observations identified through Exploratory Data Analysis and translates them into considerations for subsequent analytical work. Findings should distinguish observations about the data from decisions that belong to later stages such as Feature Engineering, Feature Selection, Model Design, or Model Evaluation. The objective is to create a concise and traceable record of what was learned, why it matters, and where the finding should be addressed. Canonical Question: What did we learn from the data, why does it matter, and what implications should be carried forward into subsequent analytical decisions?''',
'High Sparsity': '''High Sparsity describes a variable in which a large proportion of observations contain zero or another value representing the absence of the measured characteristic. Sparsity is relevant because highly sparse variables may provide information for only a small portion of the population, can create highly concentrated distributions, and may behave differently across analytical methods. A threshold of 90% or greater zero values is a reasonable general-purpose starting point for flagging a variable as highly sparse, while 70% to 90% may warrant consideration depending on the analytical context. These thresholds should be treated as review guidance rather than exclusion criteria, as a highly sparse variable may still contain important information, particularly when the relatively uncommon occurrence itself is meaningful.''',
'High Skew': '''High Skew describes a variable with a substantially asymmetric distribution, where observations are concentrated toward one end of the distribution with a longer tail extending in the other direction. Skewness is relevant because extreme asymmetry can affect statistical summaries, distance calculations, coefficient-based models, and other analytical methods, and may indicate that transformation should later be considered. An absolute skewness value below approximately 1 is generally considered relatively limited, values between 1 and 2 indicate meaningful skewness, and an absolute value of 2 or greater provides a reasonable threshold for flagging high skew for review. Skewness is not inherently problematic and should not automatically trigger transformation, particularly for financial and behavioural variables where strongly skewed distributions may accurately represent the underlying population.''',
'No Variation': '''No Variation describes a variable in which all observed values are identical, resulting in a standard deviation of zero. It is relevant because a constant variable provides no information for differentiating observations and therefore generally provides no analytical or predictive value to a model. A standard deviation of exactly zero is used as the threshold, making this a deterministic condition rather than a subjective review threshold. Variables identified as having no variation should generally be considered for exclusion, although the variable should first be reviewed to confirm that the condition is expected rather than the result of a data or preparation issue.'''
'Missingness':'''Confirm that missing values and their patterns are understood. Missingness can distort distributions and relationships, and systematic missingness may itself represent information about the population.''',
'Zeros and Sparsity':'''Confirm that concentrations of zero values are understood and that zero represents what is assumed. High sparsity can limit differentiation across the population and can cause aggregate statistical properties to obscure meaningful behaviour among non-zero observations.''',
'Distribution Shape':'''Assess whether unusual distributional characteristics such as skewness, long tails, multimodality, or concentration are genuine characteristics of the population. Distributional characteristics may materially affect transformations, statistical measures, distance-based methods, and model selection.''',
'Extreme Values':'''Identify observations substantially different from the majority of the population and determine whether they represent legitimate observations or potential data issues. Extreme values can materially influence summary statistics and some analytical methods even when they are valid.''',
'Variation':'''Confirm that variables contain sufficient variation to provide useful information. Constant variables provide no differentiation, while highly concentrated variables may provide useful information only for relatively small portions of the population.''',
'Scale':'''Understand material differences in the magnitude and units of numeric variables. Differences in scale can cause some variables to disproportionately influence distance-based, variance-based, and coefficient-based analytical methods unless appropriately addressed later.''',
'Cardinality':'''Assess the number and concentration of distinct values within categorical and discrete variables. Very high or very low cardinality can affect how variables should subsequently be represented and may reveal identifiers, excessive granularity, or limited information.''',
'Population Coverage':'''Determine whether variables meaningfully represent the full analytical population or only particular subgroups. A variable can appear statistically weak across the overall population while containing meaningful information for the population to which it actually applies.''',
'Redundancy':'''Assess whether multiple variables represent substantially the same information through correlation, repeated measurements, related timeframes, mathematical construction, or conceptual overlap. Large numbers of variables do not necessarily represent equally large amounts of independent information.''',
'Unexpected Relationships':'''Investigate relationships between variables that are substantially stronger, weaker, or different from what their definitions would reasonably suggest. Unexpected relationships can reveal useful structure but can also expose calculation errors, duplicated information, leakage, or misunderstood variables.''',
'Temporal Integrity':'''Confirm that observed patterns are consistent with the intended timing of the analytical problem and that information from the future has not inadvertently entered explanatory variables. Particular attention should be given to calculated changes, rolling measures, and variables derived from multiple observation periods.''',
'Population and Sampling':'''Confirm that the analytical population reasonably represents the population about which conclusions will be made. Filtering, exclusions, unavailable records, or sampling decisions can create patterns that reflect dataset construction rather than the underlying population.''',
'Leakage':'''Assess whether any variable directly or indirectly contains information that would not legitimately be available when the model is intended to operate. Leakage can produce apparently strong analytical relationships that will not generalize when the model is used.''',
'Plausibility':'''Apply domain knowledge to determine whether observed values, distributions, and relationships are reasonably plausible. Statistically valid calculations can still reveal incorrect data when their results are inconsistent with how the underlying business or real-world process operates.''',
'Stability':'''Where multiple periods or populations are available, assess whether important statistical characteristics remain reasonably stable. Large unexplained changes in distributions, prevalence, or relationships may indicate population changes, data-generation changes, or unstable analytical information.''',

'Statistical Property Assessment': '''Statistical Property Assessment is the systematic evaluation and interpretation of statistical characteristics identified during analysis to determine their analytical relevance. Statistical properties and appropriate measures will vary depending on the type of analysis, but may include characteristics such as distribution, variation, sparsity, skewness, correlation, association, or dependence. The purpose is not to classify statistical characteristics as inherently good or bad, but to understand what is driving them, whether they meaningfully represent the underlying data, and whether they create considerations for subsequent analytical decisions. Canonical Question: What do the observed statistical properties tell us about the information being analyzed, and what implications, if any, should be carried forward?''',
# SPA STEPS
'Validity': '''Validity assesses whether an observed statistical property reflects a genuine characteristic of the underlying data or could be the result of a data issue. Unexpected properties may arise from errors in collection, extraction, transformation, coding, or interpretation rather than the phenomenon being measured. Establishing validity prevents potentially erroneous characteristics from being accepted as meaningful analytical information. Canonical Question: Is this property real, or could it result from a data issue?''',
'Cause': '''Cause examines what characteristic or structure in the underlying data is producing the observed statistical property. This involves moving beyond the statistical measure itself to understand the observations, values, frequencies, or patterns responsible for it. Understanding the cause helps distinguish an interpretable data characteristic from a statistical result that requires further investigation. Canonical Question: What characteristic of the underlying data is producing it?''',
'Meaning': '''Meaning assesses whether the characteristic producing the statistical property has a sensible real-world or domain interpretation. A statistical pattern may be valid while still being difficult to explain in the context of what the variable or information represents. Establishing meaning provides confidence that the observed behaviour can be understood in relation to the underlying phenomenon. Canonical Question: Does that characteristic have a meaningful real-world interpretation?''',
'Materiality': '''Materiality assesses whether the observed statistical property is substantial enough to warrant analytical attention. Statistical characteristics can exist without being sufficiently pronounced, prevalent, or consequential to influence how the information should be understood or subsequently analyzed. Materiality helps distinguish properties that should be carried forward from those that can reasonably be acknowledged without further consideration. Canonical Question: Is it significant enough to matter analytically?''',
'Implication': '''Implication identifies whether the statistical property creates a consideration for subsequent stages of analysis. The objective is not to determine the downstream analytical treatment at this stage, but to ensure potentially relevant characteristics are not lost as the analysis progresses. Implications may relate to Feature Engineering, Feature Selection, Model Selection, analytical interpretation, or other subsequent decisions. Canonical Question: Could it affect subsequent Feature Engineering, Feature Selection, Model Selection, or interpretation?''',
'Disposition': '''Disposition determines how the statistical finding should be handled once it has been assessed. A finding may be accepted when it is sufficiently understood and requires no additional investigation, investigated when its validity, cause, or meaning remains uncertain, or carried forward when it represents a legitimate characteristic with potential downstream analytical relevance. Disposition provides an explicit conclusion to the assessment without prematurely determining the eventual analytical treatment. Canonical Question: What should be done with the finding: Accept, Investigate, or Carry Forward?''',
'Bivariate Analysis': '''Bivariate Analysis is the examination of the relationship between two variables to understand how they vary, differ, or occur together. The appropriate analytical method depends on the types of variables being compared and may include correlation, association, group comparisons, cross-tabulation, or other measures of dependence and relationship. The purpose at this stage is to identify and understand meaningful, unexpected, redundant, or potentially problematic relationships in the data rather than determine their predictive value or suitability for a particular model. Canonical Question: What relationships exist between variables, do those relationships make intuitive sense, and what implications, if any, should be carried forward?'''

"Relationship Breadth":"The extent to which a member maintains relationships across different products, services, and capabilities offered by the organization. Breadth reflects the range of the relationship rather than the financial size or intensity of any individual component. How broad is the member's relationship with us?",
'Relationship Depth':"The extent to which the member meaningfully uses or maintains the products and services they hold. Depth distinguishes merely having a relationship from having a substantive relationship within those products or services. How deeply does the member use what they have with us?",
'Behaviour':"The frequency, consistency, and nature of the member's observable interactions with the organization. This captures how actively the member uses the organization as part of their ongoing financial activity. How actively does the member interact with us?",
'Trajectory':"The direction and evolution of the member's relationship over time. Trajectory captures whether engagement appears to be strengthening, stable, weakening, or otherwise changing rather than relying only on the member's current state.  How is the member's relationship with us changing?",
"Current Known Financial Profile":"Represents the member's financial position as it can currently be observed or reasonably estimated from available information, including known financial holdings, wealth indicators, and their existing financial relationship with the organization. It provides a structured representation of what is currently known about the member's financial position while explicitly recognizing that the information may be incomplete, estimated, or subject to data-quality limitations. It should not be interpreted as a complete representation of the member's wealth, financial capacity, needs, or intentions. What do we currently know about the member's financial position?",
